Your goal is to answer the doctor's question naturally, using the\ninformation you are given.\n\nAnswer with the [Targeted Info], told in the way [Conversation Style] describes.\n\n[Dialogue History]\n(this is the start of the consultation)\n\n[Last Question]\nHello, and welcome. I'm Dr. [Name], a psychiatrist. What brings you in to see me today?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 57 / customer_service_representative / high_school\n- severity: Mild\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Constant_Activity\nmanifestation: \"What brings me in is that I feel like I’m always moving from one thing to the next, like I can’t just idle, and the new work setup has made that a lot harder to hide.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Verbose patient.\nA verbose patient may\n1) provide excessively detailed responses when describing their symptoms and experiences, even when the details are directly relevant,\n2) elaborate extensively on the background, events, thoughts, feelings, and personal experiences surrounding their symptoms, and\n3) demonstrate difficulty allowing the doctor to guide the interview or move on to another clinical topic.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, Dr. [Name], thank you for seeing me. It's, well, it's a bit of a story, I suppose. I've been a customer service rep for years, mostly on the floor, you know, walking around, helping folks out. But recently, they changed our whole setup. Now we're tied to these desks, headsets on, staring at screens all day. And, honestly, it's driving me up the wall. I've always been the kind of guy who just... goes. I'm always moving from one thing to the next, fixing this, checking on that. I can't just idle, you know? It's like I have this motor running inside me that won't shut off. When I was on my feet, I could hide it, or at least put it to good use. But now, sitting there for eight hours... it's like trying to hold a lid on a boiling pot. I'm tapping my feet, I'm rearranging my pens, I'm getting up to get water every ten minutes. My supervisor even pulled me aside last week and asked if I was okay because I looked so antsy. So, I guess what brings me in is that I just can't seem to sit still anymore, and this new work setup has made that a lot harder to hide. It's really starting to become a problem.

I'm Dr. [Name], a psychiatrist. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well, Dr. [Name], thank you for seeing me. It's, well, it's a bit of a story, I suppose. Now we're tied to these desks, headsets on, staring at screens all day. And, honestly, it's driving me up the wall. I've always been the kind of guy who just... goes. I'm always moving from one thing to the next, fixing this, checking on that. I can't just idle, you know? It's like I have this motor running inside me that won't shut off. So, I guess what brings me in is that I just can't seem to sit still anymore, and this new work setup has made that a lot harder to hide. It's really starting to become a problem.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }

```json
{
  "candidates": ["F90.9", "F41.1", "F41.9", "F32.9", "F60.3"],
  "note": "Initial presentation suggests psychomotor restlessness and difficulty with sedentary tasks. Key features include: lifelong pattern of high activity/movement preference, motor restlessness (foot tapping, frequent position changes), difficulty remaining still, and recent symptom exacerbation with environmental change (desk-bound work). Differential includes ADHD (F90.9) as primary consideration given apparent lifelong pattern, generalized anxiety disorder (F41.9) and adjustment disorder with anxiety (F41.1) given recent stressor and anxiety symptoms, depressive disorder (F32.9) as restlessness can present atypically in depression, and obsessive-compulsive personality disorder (F60.3) given apparent need for activity and control.",
  "is_final": false
}
```

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, you know, when you put it like that... I guess I’ve been blaming the new desk setup so much because it’s the thing that's right in front of my face every day right now, but... looking back? No, no, this was around long, long before this job change. I mean, we are talking way back. 

If I really sit and think about it, going all the way back to grade school... boy, my mother used to dread parent-teacher conferences, she really did. She kept all my old report cards in this wooden chest at the end of the hallway, and it was always the exact same story every single year. The teachers—I remember Mrs. Gable in fourth grade especially, very strict lady—they always wrote that I was a bright boy, which was nice of them to say, I suppose, but it was always, always followed by a "but." Bright, but careless. That was the word they loved to use. Careless. Because I’d just breeze through whatever spelling test or math worksheet they handed out just so I could be done and do something else, and I'd make all these silly mistakes. 

And the talking! Oh, I was a talker. I constantly talked out of turn. I’d just blurt out the answer if I knew it, or I’d turn around and start a whole conversation with the poor kid sitting behind me while the teacher was still up at the chalkboard trying to explain the lesson. They were always, and I mean constantly, having to remind me to just stay with my work. "Eyes on your own paper," "turn back around," "focus on your assignment"—it was like a broken record in those classrooms. I just couldn't stay glued to the paper. 

So, honestly, the more I talk about it with you, this... this motor I was mentioning earlier? It’s been running since I was in short pants. It’s just that, for a long time, my jobs and my life sort of let me get away with it, or put it to work, you know? Until they stuck me at this desk.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, yes, absolutely. Focusing has always been... well, it's always been a bit of a moving target for me, if I'm being completely honest. Even now, it's not just the sitting still that gets me. Like, they have these coaching meetings at work—these long, drawn-out sessions where the supervisor sits down with you to go over your call metrics, or they hand out these new procedural manuals that are thick as a phone book. And I always go in with the best of intentions, you know? I sit down, I tell myself, "Okay, pay attention, this is important," and I start off just fine. I really do. For the first few minutes, I'm right there with them. 

But then... it's almost like my brain just slips a gear. The supervisor will be talking, or I'll be reading down the page of this new procedure, and my eyes are still moving across the words, or I'm still nodding at the supervisor, but suddenly I realize I haven't actually taken in a single thing that was just said or read for the last five minutes. My mind has just completely wandered off to what I'm having for lunch, or a noise out in the hallway, or fixing that squeaky hinge on my back door at home. And then I have to sort of mentally scramble to catch up, or go back and re-read the same paragraph three times just to figure out what it's actually telling me to do. It’s incredibly frustrating, because I want to pay attention, I really do, but it just feels like my attention span has a strict time limit before it just packs up and leaves the room without me noticing.

Now we're tied to these desks, headsets on, staring at screens all day. And, honestly, it's driving me up the wall. I've always been the kind of guy who just... goes. When I was on my feet, I could hide it, or at least put it to good use. But now, sitting there for eight hours... it's like trying to hold a lid on a boiling pot. I'm tapping my feet, I'm rearranging my pens, I'm getting up to get water every ten minutes. My supervisor even pulled me aside last week and asked if I was okay because I looked so antsy. So, I guess what brings me in is that I just can't seem to sit still anymore, and this new work setup has made that a lot harder to hide. It's really starting to become a problem.\n\nDoctor: You mentioned you've always been someone who needs to keep moving and can't idle. Going back to when you were a kid or a teenager, were you always like this, or is this something that's gotten worse more recently?\n\nPatient: Well, you know, when you put it like that... I guess I’ve been blaming the new desk setup so much because it’s the thing that's right in front of my face every day right now, but... looking back? No, no, this was around long, long before this job change. I mean, we are talking way back. \n\nIf I really sit and think about it, going all the way back to grade school... boy, my mother used to dread parent-teacher conferences, she really did. She kept all my old report cards in this wooden chest at the end of the hallway, and it was always the exact same story every single year. The teachers—I remember Mrs. Gable in fourth grade especially, very strict lady—they always wrote that I was a bright boy, which was nice of them to say, I suppose, but it was always, always followed by a \"but.\" Bright, but careless. That was the word they loved to use. Careless. Because I’d just breeze through whatever spelling test or math worksheet they handed out just so I could be done and do something else, and I'd make all these silly mistakes. \n\nAnd the talking! Oh, I was a talker. I constantly talked out of turn. I’d just blurt out the answer if I knew it, or I’d turn around and start a whole conversation with the poor kid sitting behind me while the teacher was still up at the chalkboard trying to explain the lesson. They were always, and I mean constantly, having to remind me to just stay with my work. \"Eyes on your own paper,\" \"turn back around,\" \"focus on your assignment\"—it was like a broken record in those classrooms. I just couldn't stay glued to the paper. \n\nSo, honestly, the more I talk about it with you, this... this motor I was mentioning earlier? It’s been running since I was in short pants. It’s just that, for a long time, my jobs and my life sort of let me get away with it, or put it to work, you know? Until they stuck me at this desk.\n\nDoctor: When you were in school, beyond just the talking out of turn and not being able to sit still, did you also have trouble focusing or keeping your mind on what you were doing? Like, would your attention wander, or did you find it hard to organize your work or remember instructions?\n\nPatient: Well, yes, absolutely. Focusing has always been... well, it's always been a bit of a moving target for me, if I'm being completely honest. Even now, it's not just the sitting still that gets me. Like, they have these coaching meetings at work—these long, drawn-out sessions where the supervisor sits down with you to go over your call metrics, or they hand out these new procedural manuals that are thick as a phone book. And I always go in with the best of intentions, you know? I sit down, I tell myself, \"Okay, pay attention, this is important,\" and I start off just fine. I really do. For the first few minutes, I'm right there with them. \n\nBut then... it's almost like my brain just slips a gear. The supervisor will be talking, or I'll be reading down the page of this new procedure, and my eyes are still moving across the words, or I'm still nodding at the supervisor, but suddenly I realize I haven't actually taken in a single thing that was just said or read for the last five minutes. My mind has just completely wandered off to what I'm having for lunch, or a noise out in the hallway, or fixing that squeaky hinge on my back door at home. And then I have to sort of mentally scramble to catch up, or go back and re-read the same paragraph three times just to figure out what it's actually telling me to do. It’s incredibly frustrating, because I want to pay attention, I really do, but it just feels like my attention span has a strict time limit before it just packs up and leaves the room without me noticing.\n\n[Last Question]\nWhen you were in school, beyond the talking out of turn and difficulty focusing, were you also constantly fidgeting, having trouble staying seated during class, or getting up and moving around when you were supposed to be sitting still? How much of a problem was that compared to the talking and attention issues?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 57 / customer_service_representative / high_school\n- severity: Mild\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Fidgeting\nmanifestation: \"Even when I’m trying to look calm, my foot is bouncing under the desk or I’m clicking a pen during meetings without really noticing it.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Verbose patient.\nA verbose patient may\n1) provide excessively detailed responses when describing their symptoms and experiences, even when the details are directly relevant,\n2) elaborate extensively on the background, events, thoughts, feelings, and personal experiences surrounding their symptoms, and\n3) demonstrate difficulty allowing the doctor to guide the interview or move on to another clinical topic.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Oh, the fidgeting. Yes. I mean, it was always there, right alongside the talking and the daydreaming. It's funny you ask how it compared, because to me, it all just felt like one big ball of nervous energy. But yes, staying seated? That was torture. Pure torture. I remember in middle school, we had these desks where the chair was attached to the table part, you know the ones? I would rock that whole contraption back and forth, back and forth, until the metal legs would squeak on the linoleum. The teachers hated it. And if I wasn't rocking, I was finding any excuse to get up. I’d drop my pencil on purpose just so I could dive under the desk to get it, or I’d ask to go to the bathroom three times before lunch, or I’d volunteer to take the eraser outside and clap the chalk dust out of it just to get out of that seat. 

And you know, even now, even when I'm really, really trying my absolute hardest to just sit there and look professional—like in those coaching meetings I mentioned, where I'm trying to look calm and collected—it's like the energy just has to leak out somewhere. If I force my body to stay in the chair, my foot is under the desk, just bouncing away, going a mile a minute. Or I'll have a pen in my hand, and I'll be clicking it. Click, click, click. I don't even realize I'm doing it until the supervisor stops talking and just stares at my hand. It's embarrassing, frankly. I’m a grown man, fifty-seven years old, and I still can't just sit still like a normal person. It’s like, if my mouth isn't moving, my foot has to be, or my fingers have to be messing with something. So, to answer your question, yes, the fidgeting was always a huge part of it, and it still is, even when I'm trying my best to hide it.

in grade school my teachers wrote that I was bright but careless, talked out of turn, and had to be reminded to stay with my work.\n- key: functional_impairment\n  name: Functional Impairment\n  description: required\n  manifestation: At work it has started showing up in my reviews, with supervisors pointing out unfinished tickets and mistakes that slow the team down even though I’m trying hard and generally get along well with customers.\n- key: duration\n  name: Duration\n  description: 1 year and 10 months\n  manifestation: It’s been noticeably harder to manage for about 1 year and 10 months, since my job shifted into the new hybrid chat and CRM role.\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 57 / customer_service_representative / high_school\n- chief_complaint_symptom_code: S014\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: You mentioned you've always been someone who needs to keep moving and can't idle. Going back to when you were a kid or a teenager, were you always like this, or is this something that's gotten worse more recently?\n\nPatient: Well, you know, when you put it like that... I guess I’ve been blaming the new desk setup so much because it’s the thing that's right in front of my face every day right now, but... looking back? No, no, this was around long, long before this job change. I mean, we are talking way back. \n\nIf I really sit and think about it, going all the way back to grade school... boy, my mother used to dread parent-teacher conferences, she really did. She kept all my old report cards in this wooden chest at the end of the hallway, and it was always the exact same story every single year. The teachers—I remember Mrs. Gable in fourth grade especially, very strict lady—they always wrote that I was a bright boy, which was nice of them to say, I suppose, but it was always, always followed by a \"but.\" Bright, but careless. That was the word they loved to use. Careless. Because I’d just breeze through whatever spelling test or math worksheet they handed out just so I could be done and do something else, and I'd make all these silly mistakes. \n\nAnd the talking! Oh, I was a talker. I constantly talked out of turn. I’d just blurt out the answer if I knew it, or I’d turn around and start a whole conversation with the poor kid sitting behind me while the teacher was still up at the chalkboard trying to explain the lesson. They were always, and I mean constantly, having to remind me to just stay with my work. \"Eyes on your own paper,\" \"turn back around,\" \"focus on your assignment\"—it was like a broken record in those classrooms. I just couldn't stay glued to the paper. \n\nSo, honestly, the more I talk about it with you, this... this motor I was mentioning earlier? It’s been running since I was in short pants. It’s just that, for a long time, my jobs and my life sort of let me get away with it, or put it to work, you know? Until they stuck me at this desk.\n\nDoctor: When you were in school, beyond just the talking out of turn and not being able to sit still, did you also have trouble focusing or keeping your mind on what you were doing? Like, would your attention wander, or did you find it hard to organize your work or remember instructions?\n\nPatient: Well, yes, absolutely. Focusing has always been... well, it's always been a bit of a moving target for me, if I'm being completely honest. Even now, it's not just the sitting still that gets me. Like, they have these coaching meetings at work—these long, drawn-out sessions where the supervisor sits down with you to go over your call metrics, or they hand out these new procedural manuals that are thick as a phone book. And I always go in with the best of intentions, you know? I sit down, I tell myself, \"Okay, pay attention, this is important,\" and I start off just fine. I really do. For the first few minutes, I'm right there with them. \n\nBut then... it's almost like my brain just slips a gear. The supervisor will be talking, or I'll be reading down the page of this new procedure, and my eyes are still moving across the words, or I'm still nodding at the supervisor, but suddenly I realize I haven't actually taken in a single thing that was just said or read for the last five minutes. My mind has just completely wandered off to what I'm having for lunch, or a noise out in the hallway, or fixing that squeaky hinge on my back door at home. And then I have to sort of mentally scramble to catch up, or go back and re-read the same paragraph three times just to figure out what it's actually telling me to do. It’s incredibly frustrating, because I want to pay attention, I really do, but it just feels like my attention span has a strict time limit before it just packs up and leaves the room without me noticing.\n\nDoctor: When you were in school, beyond the talking out of turn and difficulty focusing, were you also constantly fidgeting, having trouble staying seated during class, or getting up and moving around when you were supposed to be sitting still? How much of a problem was that compared to the talking and attention issues?\n\nPatient: Oh, the fidgeting. Yes. I mean, it was always there, right alongside the talking and the daydreaming. It's funny you ask how it compared, because to me, it all just felt like one big ball of nervous energy. But yes, staying seated? That was torture. Pure torture. I remember in middle school, we had these desks where the chair was attached to the table part, you know the ones? I would rock that whole contraption back and forth, back and forth, until the metal legs would squeak on the linoleum. The teachers hated it. And if I wasn't rocking, I was finding any excuse to get up. I’d drop my pencil on purpose just so I could dive under the desk to get it, or I’d ask to go to the bathroom three times before lunch, or I’d volunteer to take the eraser outside and clap the chalk dust out of it just to get out of that seat. \n\nAnd you know, even now, even when I'm really, really trying my absolute hardest to just sit there and look professional—like in those coaching meetings I mentioned, where I'm trying to look calm and collected—it's like the energy just has to leak out somewhere. If I force my body to stay in the chair, my foot is under the desk, just bouncing away, going a mile a minute. Or I'll have a pen in my hand, and I'll be clicking it. Click, click, click. I don't even realize I'm doing it until the supervisor stops talking and just stares at my hand. It's embarrassing, frankly. I’m a grown man, fifty-seven years old, and I still can't just sit still like a normal person. It’s like, if my mouth isn't moving, my foot has to be, or my fingers have to be messing with something. So, to answer your question, yes, the fidgeting was always a huge part of it, and it still is, even when I'm trying my best to hide it.\n\n[Last Question]\nLooking back at your school years, would you say that this constant motion, the trouble focusing, and the blurting things out were causing real problems for you—like did it affect your grades, your friendships, or get you into trouble with teachers and parents? Or was it more just an annoyance that people commented on?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. If the question touches several topics, return the one it is\n  most directly about — never more than one.\n- Copy `key`, `name` and `description` exactly as they appear in [Symptom Profiles].\n- If your profile does not cover what the doctor asked, return the text \"None\" for `key`,\n  `name` and `description`, and say why in `reason`. Do NOT force an unrelated item.\n- On your first reply of the consultation, always return the item whose key is your\n  chief_complaint_symptom_code, whatever the doctor opened with.\n- Do not answer the doctor, and do not write anything outside the JSON.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"key\": \"<key>\", \"name\": \"<name>\", \"description\": \"<description>\", \"reason\": \"<why this item>\"}\n"
  },

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, looking back at school... I mean, it was definitely more than just a little annoyance, though I wouldn't say I was a juvenile delinquent or anything like that. It was more like... a constant friction, you know? Like I said about the report cards, my grades always took a hit because of the silly mistakes, not because I didn't understand the material. And I spent more than my fair share of time clapping those erasers or sitting in detention because I just couldn't keep my mouth shut. My friends usually thought I was a riot, but the teachers... yeah, I was a headache for them. My poor mother got a lot of phone calls.

But you know, you get through school, you get out into the real world, and you figure out ways to cope. Or at least, you find jobs where it doesn't matter as much, where you're on your feet and moving. But now? Now it's becoming a very, very real problem. That's the whole reason I finally decided I needed to talk to somebody. Because at this job, sitting at this desk, it's actually starting to show up in my official performance reviews. 

And I'll tell you, it breaks my heart a little bit, because I am really, really trying. I put in the effort every single day. And the funny thing is, I am great with the customers. I really am! I can talk to anybody, I can calm down a caller who's been on hold for twenty minutes, we end up having a nice chat about their grandkids or the weather in Ohio, and we get along just fine. My customer satisfaction scores are always great. 

But then... there's the administrative side of it. The tickets. We have this whole computerized ticketing system, and my supervisors keep pulling me into those little glass offices and pointing out that I have all these unfinished tickets. I'll take a call, resolve the issue, but then I'll get distracted before I finish typing up the notes, or I'll skip a step in the logging process. Just these careless mistakes, exactly like the ones Mrs. Gable used to yell at me for. But here, it messes up the whole workflow. Because then the next shift comes in, or another department gets the ticket, and they have to stop what they're doing to figure out what I did wrong or fill in the blanks I left. 

So it ends up slowing the entire team down. And it's just so incredibly frustrating to sit in those reviews and hear them tell me I'm holding everyone back, especially when I'm putting in so much effort and doing so well with the actual people on the phone. It's like I can do the hard part—talking to the angry customers—but the supposedly easy part, just filling out the form and finishing the ticket, is what's tripping me up and threatening my job. So, yes, it's causing real problems now. Very real problems.

Because at this job, sitting at this desk, it's actually starting to show up in my official performance reviews. \n\nAnd I'll tell you, it breaks my heart a little bit, because I am really, really trying. I put in the effort every single day. And the funny thing is, I am great with the customers. I really am! I can talk to anybody, I can calm down a caller who's been on hold for twenty minutes, we end up having a nice chat about their grandkids or the weather in Ohio, and we get along just fine. My customer satisfaction scores are always great. \n\nBut then... there's the administrative side of it. The tickets. We have this whole computerized ticketing system, and my supervisors keep pulling me into those little glass offices and pointing out that I have all these unfinished tickets. I'll take a call, resolve the issue, but then I'll get distracted before I finish typing up the notes, or I'll skip a step in the logging process. Just these careless mistakes, exactly like the ones Mrs. Gable used to yell at me for. But here, it messes up the whole workflow. Because then the next shift comes in, or another department gets the ticket, and they have to stop what they're doing to figure out what I did wrong or fill in the blanks I left. \n\nSo it ends up slowing the entire team down. And it's just so incredibly frustrating to sit in those reviews and hear them tell me I'm holding everyone back, especially when I'm putting in so much effort and doing so well with the actual people on the phone. It's like I can do the hard part—talking to the angry customers—but the supposedly easy part, just filling out the form and finishing the ticket, is what's tripping me up and threatening my job. So, yes, it's causing real problems now. Very real problems.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },

    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }

Oh, I was a talker. I constantly talked out of turn. I’d just blurt out the answer if I knew it, or I’d turn around and start a whole conversation with the poor kid sitting behind me while the teacher was still up at the chalkboard trying to explain the lesson. They were always, and I mean constantly, having to remind me to just stay with my work. \"Eyes on your own paper,\" \"turn back around,\" \"focus on your assignment\"—it was like a broken record in those classrooms. I just couldn't stay glued to the paper. \n\nSo, honestly, the more I talk about it with you, this... this motor I was mentioning earlier? It’s been running since I was in short pants. I would rock that whole contraption back and forth, back and forth, until the metal legs would squeak on the linoleum. The teachers hated it. And if I wasn't rocking, I was finding any excuse to get up. I’d drop my pencil on purpose just so I could dive under the desk to get it, or I’d ask to go to the bathroom three times before lunch, or I’d volunteer to take the eraser outside and clap the chalk dust out of it just to get out of that seat. \n\nAnd you know, even now, even when I'm really, really trying my absolute hardest to just sit there and look professional—like in those coaching meetings I mentioned, where I'm trying to look calm and collected—it's like the energy just has to leak out somewhere. If I force my body to stay in the chair, my foot is under the desk, just bouncing away, going a mile a minute. Or I'll have a pen in my hand, and I'll be clicking it. Click, click, click. I don't even realize I'm doing it until the supervisor stops talking and just stares at my hand. It's embarrassing, frankly. I’m a grown man, fifty-seven years old, and I still can't just sit still like a normal person. It’s like, if my mouth isn't moving, my foot has to be, or my fingers have to be messing with something. So, to answer your question, yes, the fidgeting was always a huge part of it, and it still is, even when I'm trying my best to hide it.\n\nDoctor: Looking back at your school years, would you say that this constant motion, the trouble focusing, and the blurting things out were causing real problems for you—like did it affect your grades, your friendships, or get you into trouble with teachers and parents? Or was it more just an annoyance that people commented on?\n\nPatient: Well, looking back at school... I mean, it was definitely more than just a little annoyance, though I wouldn't say I was a juvenile delinquent or anything like that. It was more like... a constant friction, you know? Like I said about the report cards, my grades always took a hit because of the silly mistakes, not because I didn't understand the material. And I spent more than my fair share of time clapping those erasers or sitting in detention because I just couldn't keep my mouth shut. My friends usually thought I was a riot, but the teachers... yeah, I was a headache for them. My poor mother got a lot of phone calls.\n\nBut you know, you get through school, you get out into the real world, and you figure out ways to cope. Or at least, you find jobs where it doesn't matter as much, where you're on your feet and moving. But now? Now it's becoming a very, very real problem. That's the whole reason I finally decided I needed to talk to somebody. Because at this job, sitting at this desk, it's actually starting to show up in my official performance reviews. \n\nAnd I'll tell you, it breaks my heart a little bit, because I am really, really trying. I put in the effort every single day. And the funny thing is, I am great with the customers. I really am! I can talk to anybody, I can calm down a caller who's been on hold for twenty minutes, we end up having a nice chat about their grandkids or the weather in Ohio, and we get along just fine. My customer satisfaction scores are always great. \n\nBut then... there's the administrative side of it. The tickets. We have this whole computerized ticketing system, and my supervisors keep pulling me into those little glass offices and pointing out that I have all these unfinished tickets. I'll take a call, resolve the issue, but then I'll get distracted before I finish typing up the notes, or I'll skip a step in the logging process. Just these careless mistakes, exactly like the ones Mrs. Gable used to yell at me for. But here, it messes up the whole workflow. Because then the next shift comes in, or another department gets the ticket, and they have to stop what they're doing to figure out what I did wrong or fill in the blanks I left. \n\nSo it ends up slowing the entire team down. And it's just so incredibly frustrating to sit in those reviews and hear them tell me I'm holding everyone back, especially when I'm putting in so much effort and doing so well with the actual people on the phone. It's like I can do the hard part—talking to the angry customers—but the supposedly easy part, just filling out the form and finishing the ticket, is what's tripping me up and threatening my job. So, yes, it's causing real problems now. Very real problems.\n\n[Last Question]\nHave you ever been evaluated by a doctor or psychiatrist for ADHD or attention problems, or have you ever been prescribed any medications like stimulants for focus or attention issues?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 57 / customer_service_representative / high_school\n- severity: Mild\n\n[Targeted Info]\nNone\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Verbose patient.\nA verbose patient may\n1) provide excessively detailed responses when describing their symptoms and experiences, even when the details are directly relevant,\n2) elaborate extensively on the background, events, thoughts, feelings, and personal experiences surrounding their symptoms, and\n3) demonstrate difficulty allowing the doctor to guide the interview or move on to another clinical topic.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
No, no, never. Never anything like that. I mean, you have to remember, when I was growing up—going through grade school in the late sixties and the seventies—they didn't really do that sort of thing. If you were bouncing off the walls or talking too much, they didn't send you to a psychiatrist or give you a pill. They just... well, they sent you to the principal's office, or they made you clap the erasers, like I said. Or they just wrote on your report card that you weren't applying yourself. 

So, no, nobody ever sat me down and said, "Let's test you for an attention problem." It just wasn't something people talked about back then, at least not in my neighborhood or my school. And then, as I got older and out into the workforce, I just sort of figured, well, this is just who I am, you know? I'm just a guy who has a little too much nervous energy and gets sidetracked easily. I never really thought of it as a medical thing that you go to a doctor for. 

So I've never been on any medications for it, no stimulants, nothing like that. Honestly, the closest thing I've ever had to medication for focus is just drinking a whole lot of black coffee to try and keep myself on track during the morning shift, but even that doesn't really do the trick when it comes to sitting down and finishing those computer tickets. It's really only been the last few months, with those performance reviews getting worse and my supervisor pulling me into that little glass office more and more, that I finally started thinking, "Okay, maybe this isn't just a personality quirk. Maybe I actually need to talk to a professional about this." 

My wife was the one who finally pushed me to make the appointment, actually. She saw how much it was weighing on me, coming home worried about my job security, and she said, "You're fifty-seven years old, you've worked hard your whole life, you shouldn't be stressing out this much about a desk job if there's something out there that can help." So, I guess to make a very long story short, no, this is the very first time I've ever sat down with a doctor to actually talk about any of this.

Now we're tied to these desks, headsets on, staring at screens all day. And, honestly, it's driving me up the wall. I've always been the kind of guy who just... goes. I'm always moving from one thing to the next, fixing this, checking on that. I can't just idle, you know? It's like I have this motor running inside me that won't shut off. When I was on my feet, I could hide it, or at least put it to good use. But now, sitting there for eight hours... it's like trying to hold a lid on a boiling pot. I'm tapping my feet, I'm rearranging my pens, I'm getting up to get water every ten minutes. My supervisor even pulled me aside last week and asked if I was okay because I looked so antsy. Focusing has always been... well, it's always been a bit of a moving target for me, if I'm being completely honest. Even now, it's not just the sitting still that gets me. Like, they have these coaching meetings at work—these long, drawn-out sessions where the supervisor sits down with you to go over your call metrics, or they hand out these new procedural manuals that are thick as a phone book. And I always go in with the best of intentions, you know? I sit down, I tell myself, \"Okay, pay attention, this is important,\" and I start off just fine. I really do. The supervisor will be talking, or I'll be reading down the page of this new procedure, and my eyes are still moving across the words, or I'm still nodding at the supervisor, but suddenly I realize I haven't actually taken in a single thing that was just said or read for the last five minutes. My mind has just completely wandered off to what I'm having for lunch, or a noise out in the hallway, or fixing that squeaky hinge on my back door at home. I remember in middle school, we had these desks where the chair was attached to the table part, you know the ones? I would rock that whole contraption back and forth, back and forth, until the metal legs would squeak on the linoleum. The teachers hated it. And if I wasn't rocking, I was finding any excuse to get up. I don't even realize I'm doing it until the supervisor stops talking and just stares at my hand. It's embarrassing, frankly. I can talk to anybody, I can calm down a caller who's been on hold for twenty minutes, we end up having a nice chat about their grandkids or the weather in Ohio, and we get along just fine. My customer satisfaction scores are always great. \n\nBut then... there's the administrative side of it. The tickets. We have this whole computerized ticketing system, and my supervisors keep pulling me into those little glass offices and pointing out that I have all these unfinished tickets. I'll take a call, resolve the issue, but then I'll get distracted before I finish typing up the notes, or I'll skip a step in the logging process. Just these careless mistakes, exactly like the ones Mrs. Gable used to yell at me for. But here, it messes up the whole workflow. Because then the next shift comes in, or another department gets the ticket, and they have to stop what they're doing to figure out what I did wrong or fill in the blanks I left. \n\nSo it ends up slowing the entire team down. And it's just so incredibly frustrating to sit in those reviews and hear them tell me I'm holding everyone back, especially when I'm putting in so much effort and doing so well with the actual people on the phone. It's like I can do the hard part—talking to the angry customers—but the supposedly easy part, just filling out the form and finishing the ticket, is what's tripping me up and threatening my job. So, yes, it's causing real problems now. Very real problems.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever been evaluated by a doctor or psychiatrist for ADHD or attention problems, or have you ever been prescribed any medications like stimulants for focus or attention issues?\n\nPatient: No, no, never. It's really only been the last few months, with those performance reviews getting worse and my supervisor pulling me into that little glass office more and more, that I finally started thinking, \"Okay, maybe this isn't just a personality quirk. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Oh, gosh, age five or six? Yeah, absolutely. It definitely didn't just start with this desk job, I can tell you that much for free. Looking back, this whole... whatever you want to call it, this restlessness, it was around long, long before this job change. I mean, it goes all the way back to the very beginning. 

I remember being in, what was it, first grade? We had those little wooden chairs, you know the ones, painted in bright primary colors, red and blue and yellow. And I just could not keep all four legs of that little red chair on the floor at the same time. It was like a physical impossibility for me. I was always tipping it back, tapping my toes, looking around at what everyone else was doing. 

And my mother, bless her heart, she kept all my old report cards in this big cardboard shoebox in the hall closet—she was sentimental like that, kept absolutely everything, every drawing, every spelling test. And if you looked at those grade school report cards, it was the exact same story every single year. The teachers would always write these little notes in the margins. They were very polite, but you could tell they were at their wit's end with me. They would always write that I was a bright boy, that I had a lot of potential, but that I was just so careless. "Careless" was the word they loved to use. Bright but careless. 

And they always, always mentioned the talking. "Talks out of turn," "Needs to learn to raise his hand," "Distracts his neighbors." It was like a broken record. And they were constantly having to remind me to stay with my work. I'd sit down, do the first two addition problems, and then I'd be looking out the window at the custodian mowing the lawn, or I'd be turning around trying to talk to the kid behind me about whatever cartoon was on TV the night before. The teacher would have to walk over, tap my desk, and say my name to get me to look back at the paper. 

So, yeah, it definitely didn't start later in life. It was right there from the very beginning, from the moment they first asked me to sit still and pay attention in a classroom. It's just that back then, it was just seen as boys being boys, or me not applying myself. But that restless, buzzing feeling where you just can't stay on track and keep your mouth shut? Oh, yeah, that's been with me since day one.

  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n7\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF90.9\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, and welcome. I'm Dr. [Name], a psychiatrist. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well, Dr. [Name], thank you for seeing me. It's, well, it's a bit of a story, I suppose. I've been a customer service rep for years, mostly on the floor, you know, walking around, helping folks out. But recently, they changed our whole setup. Now we're tied to these desks, headsets on, staring at screens all day. And, honestly, it's driving me up the wall. I've always been the kind of guy who just... goes. I'm always moving from one thing to the next, fixing this, checking on that. I can't just idle, you know? It's like I have this motor running inside me that won't shut off. When I was on my feet, I could hide it, or at least put it to good use. But now, sitting there for eight hours... it's like trying to hold a lid on a boiling pot. I'm tapping my feet, I'm rearranging my pens, I'm getting up to get water every ten minutes. My supervisor even pulled me aside last week and asked if I was okay because I looked so antsy. So, I guess what brings me in is that I just can't seem to sit still anymore, and this new work setup has made that a lot harder to hide. It's really starting to become a problem.\n\nDoctor: You mentioned you've always been someone who needs to keep moving and can't idle. Going back to when you were a kid or a teenager, were you always like this, or is this something that's gotten worse more recently?\n\nPatient: Well, you know, when you put it like that... No, no, this was around long, long before this job change. The teachers—I remember Mrs. Gable in fourth grade especially, very strict lady—they always wrote that I was a bright boy, which was nice of them to say, I suppose, but it was always, always followed by a \"but.\" Bright, but careless. Oh, I was a talker. I constantly talked out of turn. I’d just blurt out the answer if I knew it, or I’d turn around and start a whole conversation with the poor kid sitting behind me while the teacher was still up at the chalkboard trying to explain the lesson. They were always, and I mean constantly, having to remind me to just stay with my work. \"Eyes on your own paper,\" \"turn back around,\" \"focus on your assignment\"—it was like a broken record in those classrooms. I just couldn't stay glued to the paper. \n\nSo, honestly, the more I talk about it with you, this... this motor I was mentioning earlier? It’s been running since I was in short pants. It’s just that, for a long time, my jobs and my life sort of let me get away with it, or put it to work, you know? Like, would your attention wander, or did you find it hard to organize your work or remember instructions?\n\nPatient: Well, yes, absolutely. Focusing has always been... well, it's always been a bit of a moving target for me, if I'm being completely honest. Even now, it's not just the sitting still that gets me. Like, they have these coaching meetings at work—these long, drawn-out sessions where the supervisor sits down with you to go over your call metrics, or they hand out these new procedural manuals that are thick as a phone book. And I always go in with the best of intentions, you know? I sit down, I tell myself, \"Okay, pay attention, this is important,\" and I start off just fine. I really do. For the first few minutes, I'm right there with them. \n\nBut then... it's almost like my brain just slips a gear. The supervisor will be talking, or I'll be reading down the page of this new procedure, and my eyes are still moving across the words, or I'm still nodding at the supervisor, but suddenly I realize I haven't actually taken in a single thing that was just said or read for the last five minutes. My mind has just completely wandered off to what I'm having for lunch, or a noise out in the hallway, or fixing that squeaky hinge on my back door at home. And then I have to sort of mentally scramble to catch up, or go back and re-read the same paragraph three times just to figure out what it's actually telling me to do. It’s incredibly frustrating, because I want to pay attention, I really do, but it just feels like my attention span has a strict time limit before it just packs up and leaves the room without me noticing.\n\nDoctor: When you were in school, beyond the talking out of turn and difficulty focusing, were you also constantly fidgeting, having trouble staying seated during class, or getting up and moving around when you were supposed to be sitting still? How much of a problem was that compared to the talking and attention issues?\n\nPatient: Oh, the fidgeting. Yes. I mean, it was always there, right alongside the talking and the daydreaming. It's funny you ask how it compared, because to me, it all just felt like one big ball of nervous energy. But yes, staying seated? That was torture. Pure torture. I remember in middle school, we had these desks where the chair was attached to the table part, you know the ones? I would rock that whole contraption back and forth, back and forth, until the metal legs would squeak on the linoleum. The teachers hated it. And if I wasn't rocking, I was finding any excuse to get up. I’d drop my pencil on purpose just so I could dive under the desk to get it, or I’d ask to go to the bathroom three times before lunch, or I’d volunteer to take the eraser outside and clap the chalk dust out of it just to get out of that seat. \n\nAnd you know, even now, even when I'm really, really trying my absolute hardest to just sit there and look professional—like in those coaching meetings I mentioned, where I'm trying to look calm and collected—it's like the energy just has to leak out somewhere. If I force my body to stay in the chair, my foot is under the desk, just bouncing away, going a mile a minute. Or I'll have a pen in my hand, and I'll be clicking it. Click, click, click. I don't even realize I'm doing it until the supervisor stops talking and just stares at my hand. It's embarrassing, frankly. I’m a grown man, fifty-seven years old, and I still can't just sit still like a normal person. It’s like, if my mouth isn't moving, my foot has to be, or my fingers have to be messing with something. So, to answer your question, yes, the fidgeting was always a huge part of it, and it still is, even when I'm trying my best to hide it.\n\nDoctor: Looking back at your school years, would you say that this constant motion, the trouble focusing, and the blurting things out were causing real problems for you—like did it affect your grades, your friendships, or get you into trouble with teachers and parents? Or was it more just an annoyance that people commented on?\n\nPatient: Well, looking back at school... I mean, it was definitely more than just a little annoyance, though I wouldn't say I was a juvenile delinquent or anything like that. It was more like... a constant friction, you know? Like I said about the report cards, my grades always took a hit because of the silly mistakes, not because I didn't understand the material. And I spent more than my fair share of time clapping those erasers or sitting in detention because I just couldn't keep my mouth shut. My friends usually thought I was a riot, but the teachers... yeah, I was a headache for them. My poor mother got a lot of phone calls.\n\nBut you know, you get through school, you get out into the real world, and you figure out ways to cope. Or at least, you find jobs where it doesn't matter as much, where you're on your feet and moving. But now? Now it's becoming a very, very real problem. That's the whole reason I finally decided I needed to talk to somebody. Because at this job, sitting at this desk, it's actually starting to show up in my official performance reviews. \n\nAnd I'll tell you, it breaks my heart a little bit, because I am really, really trying. I put in the effort every single day. And the funny thing is, I am great with the customers. I really am! I can talk to anybody, I can calm down a caller who's been on hold for twenty minutes, we end up having a nice chat about their grandkids or the weather in Ohio, and we get along just fine. My customer satisfaction scores are always great. \n\nBut then... there's the administrative side of it. The tickets. We have this whole computerized ticketing system, and my supervisors keep pulling me into those little glass offices and pointing out that I have all these unfinished tickets. I'll take a call, resolve the issue, but then I'll get distracted before I finish typing up the notes, or I'll skip a step in the logging process. Just these careless mistakes, exactly like the ones Mrs. Gable used to yell at me for. But here, it messes up the whole workflow. Because then the next shift comes in, or another department gets the ticket, and they have to stop what they're doing to figure out what I did wrong or fill in the blanks I left. \n\nSo it ends up slowing the entire team down. And it's just so incredibly frustrating to sit in those reviews and hear them tell me I'm holding everyone back, especially when I'm putting in so much effort and doing so well with the actual people on the phone. It's like I can do the hard part—talking to the angry customers—but the supposedly easy part, just filling out the form and finishing the ticket, is what's tripping me up and threatening my job. So, yes, it's causing real problems now. Very real problems.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever been evaluated by a doctor or psychiatrist for ADHD or attention problems, or have you ever been prescribed any medications like stimulants for focus or attention issues?\n\nPatient: No, no, never. Never anything like that. I mean, you have to remember, when I was growing up—going through grade school in the late sixties and the seventies—they didn't really do that sort of thing. If you were bouncing off the walls or talking too much, they didn't send you to a psychiatrist or give you a pill. They just... well, they sent you to the principal's office, or they made you clap the erasers, like I said. Or they just wrote on your report card that you weren't applying yourself. \n\nSo, no, nobody ever sat me down and said, \"Let's test you for an attention problem.\" It just wasn't something people talked about back then, at least not in my neighborhood or my school. And then, as I got older and out into the workforce, I just sort of figured, well, this is just who I am, you know? I'm just a guy who has a little too much nervous energy and gets sidetracked easily. I never really thought of it as a medical thing that you go to a doctor for. \n\nSo I've never been on any medications for it, no stimulants, nothing like that. Honestly, the closest thing I've ever had to medication for focus is just drinking a whole lot of black coffee to try and keep myself on track during the morning shift, but even that doesn't really do the trick when it comes to sitting down and finishing those computer tickets. It's really only been the last few months, with those performance reviews getting worse and my supervisor pulling me into that little glass office more and more, that I finally started thinking, \"Okay, maybe this isn't just a personality quirk. Maybe I actually need to talk to a professional about this.\" \n\nMy wife was the one who finally pushed me to make the appointment, actually. She saw how much it was weighing on me, coming home worried about my job security, and she said, \"You're fifty-seven years old, you've worked hard your whole life, you shouldn't be stressing out this much about a desk job if there's something out there that can help.\" So, I guess to make a very long story short, no, this is the very first time I've ever sat down with a doctor to actually talk about any of this.\n\nDoctor: When you think back to your earliest memories—say, around age five or six, when you first started school—do you remember having this same restless feeling and trouble sitting still, or did it start a bit later?\n\nPatient: Oh, gosh, age five or six? Yeah, absolutely. It definitely didn't just start with this desk job, I can tell you that much for free. Looking back, this whole... whatever you want to call it, this restlessness, it was around long, long before this job change. I mean, it goes all the way back to the very beginning. \n\nI remember being in, what was it, first grade? We had those little wooden chairs, you know the ones, painted in bright primary colors, red and blue and yellow. And I just could not keep all four legs of that little red chair on the floor at the same time. It was like a physical impossibility for me. I was always tipping it back, tapping my toes, looking around at what everyone else was doing. \n\nAnd my mother, bless her heart, she kept all my old report cards in this big cardboard shoebox in the hall closet—she was sentimental like that, kept absolutely everything, every drawing, every spelling test. And if you looked at those grade school report cards, it was the exact same story every single year. The teachers would always write these little notes in the margins. They were very polite, but you could tell they were at their wit's end with me. They would always write that I was a bright boy, that I had a lot of potential, but that I was just so careless. \"Careless\" was the word they loved to use. Bright but careless. \n\nAnd they always, always mentioned the talking. \"Talks out of turn,\" \"Needs to learn to raise his hand,\" \"Distracts his neighbors.\" It was like a broken record. And they were constantly having to remind me to stay with my work. I'd sit down, do the first two addition problems, and then I'd be looking out the window at the custodian mowing the lawn, or I'd be turning around trying to talk to the kid behind me about whatever cartoon was on TV the night before. The teacher would have to walk over, tap my desk, and say my name to get me to look back at the paper. \n\nSo, yeah, it definitely didn't start later in life. It was right there from the very beginning, from the moment they first asked me to sit still and pay attention in a classroom. It's just that back then, it was just seen as boys being boys, or me not applying myself. But that restless, buzzing feeling where you just can't stay on track and keep your mouth shut? Oh, yeah, that's been with me since day one.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

    "content": "Return the JSON now."

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, that's a really good question, because for the longest time, it wasn't really a *problem* problem, if you know what I mean. Like I said, I'd always just been the energetic guy. For decades, I worked jobs where I could move around, or if I was on the phones, I could pace back and forth with a headset, doodle on a notepad, you name it. The restlessness was always there, just sort of humming in the background, but I managed it. I really did. I figured out how to make it work for me.

But then... gosh, let me think about the exact timeline. It’s been noticeably harder to manage for about, I'd say, a year and ten months now. Yeah, almost exactly a year and ten months. And I know the exact timeframe because that’s when the company decided to do this massive overhaul of our whole department. They brought in all these corporate consultants, and they completely shifted my job into this new—what do they call it—a hybrid chat and CRM role. CRM stands for Customer Relationship Management, in case you aren't familiar with the corporate lingo. It's this incredibly complicated, layered software system. 

So, instead of just talking to a nice lady on the phone and jotting down a quick note like I used to do for years, suddenly I'm supposed to be monitoring two different live chat windows on my left monitor, keeping the main phone line open on my headset, and simultaneously logging every single tiny detail of the interaction into this CRM database on my right monitor. And there are all these drop-down menus, and required fields, and specific tags you have to apply. If you miss one little checkbox, the whole ticket bounces back to you or gets lost in the ether. 

And that's when the wheels really started to come off the wagon for me. A year and ten months of just... well, drowning in it, to be perfectly honest. Because before that, I could rely on my gift of gab and my people skills to get me through. But this new hybrid setup, it demands a kind of sitting still, staring at a screen, and juggling tiny, boring details that my brain just absolutely rebels against. I try to look at the chat window, and then the phone beeps, and then I forget which drop-down menu I was in, and I end up clicking the wrong thing, and it's just a mess. 

So, yes, the restless feeling and the trouble focusing, that's always been with me, sure. But it wasn't threatening my livelihood until they switched up my job into this hybrid CRM thing a year and ten months ago. It's just a whole different ballgame now, and I just can't seem to get the hang of it no matter how hard I try, which is why those performance reviews have been tanking. It's like they took the one thing I'm bad at and made it my entire job description...
