[
  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n8\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF90.9\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, and welcome. I'm Dr. [Name], a psychiatrist. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well, Dr. [Name], thank you for seeing me. It's, well, it's a bit of a story, I suppose. I've been a customer service rep for years, mostly on the floor, you know, walking around, helping folks out. But recently, they changed our whole setup. Now we're tied to these desks, headsets on, staring at screens all day. And, honestly, it's driving me up the wall. I've always been the kind of guy who just... goes. I'm always moving from one thing to the next, fixing this, checking on that. I can't just idle, you know? It's like I have this motor running inside me that won't shut off. When I was on my feet, I could hide it, or at least put it to good use. But now, sitting there for eight hours... it's like trying to hold a lid on a boiling pot. I'm tapping my feet, I'm rearranging my pens, I'm getting up to get water every ten minutes. My supervisor even pulled me aside last week and asked if I was okay because I looked so antsy. So, I guess what brings me in is that I just can't seem to sit still anymore, and this new work setup has made that a lot harder to hide. It's really starting to become a problem.\n\nDoctor: You mentioned you've always been someone who needs to keep moving and can't idle. Going back to when you were a kid or a teenager, were you always like this, or is this something that's gotten worse more recently?\n\nPatient: Well, you know, when you put it like that... I guess I’ve been blaming the new desk setup so much because it’s the thing that's right in front of my face every day right now, but... looking back? No, no, this was around long, long before this job change. I mean, we are talking way back. \n\nIf I really sit and think about it, going all the way back to grade school... boy, my mother used to dread parent-teacher conferences, she really did. She kept all my old report cards in this wooden chest at the end of the hallway, and it was always the exact same story every single year. The teachers—I remember Mrs. Gable in fourth grade especially, very strict lady—they always wrote that I was a bright boy, which was nice of them to say, I suppose, but it was always, always followed by a \"but.\" Bright, but careless. That was the word they loved to use. Careless. Because I’d just breeze through whatever spelling test or math worksheet they handed out just so I could be done and do something else, and I'd make all these silly mistakes. \n\nAnd the talking! Oh, I was a talker. I constantly talked out of turn. I’d just blurt out the answer if I knew it, or I’d turn around and start a whole conversation with the poor kid sitting behind me while the teacher was still up at the chalkboard trying to explain the lesson. They were always, and I mean constantly, having to remind me to just stay with my work. \"Eyes on your own paper,\" \"turn back around,\" \"focus on your assignment\"—it was like a broken record in those classrooms. I just couldn't stay glued to the paper. \n\nSo, honestly, the more I talk about it with you, this... this motor I was mentioning earlier? It’s been running since I was in short pants. It’s just that, for a long time, my jobs and my life sort of let me get away with it, or put it to work, you know? Until they stuck me at this desk.\n\nDoctor: When you were in school, beyond just the talking out of turn and not being able to sit still, did you also have trouble focusing or keeping your mind on what you were doing? Like, would your attention wander, or did you find it hard to organize your work or remember instructions?\n\nPatient: Well, yes, absolutely. Focusing has always been... well, it's always been a bit of a moving target for me, if I'm being completely honest. Even now, it's not just the sitting still that gets me. Like, they have these coaching meetings at work—these long, drawn-out sessions where the supervisor sits down with you to go over your call metrics, or they hand out these new procedural manuals that are thick as a phone book. And I always go in with the best of intentions, you know? I sit down, I tell myself, \"Okay, pay attention, this is important,\" and I start off just fine. I really do. For the first few minutes, I'm right there with them. \n\nBut then... it's almost like my brain just slips a gear. The supervisor will be talking, or I'll be reading down the page of this new procedure, and my eyes are still moving across the words, or I'm still nodding at the supervisor, but suddenly I realize I haven't actually taken in a single thing that was just said or read for the last five minutes. My mind has just completely wandered off to what I'm having for lunch, or a noise out in the hallway, or fixing that squeaky hinge on my back door at home. And then I have to sort of mentally scramble to catch up, or go back and re-read the same paragraph three times just to figure out what it's actually telling me to do. It’s incredibly frustrating, because I want to pay attention, I really do, but it just feels like my attention span has a strict time limit before it just packs up and leaves the room without me noticing.\n\nDoctor: When you were in school, beyond the talking out of turn and difficulty focusing, were you also constantly fidgeting, having trouble staying seated during class, or getting up and moving around when you were supposed to be sitting still? How much of a problem was that compared to the talking and attention issues?\n\nPatient: Oh, the fidgeting. Yes. I mean, it was always there, right alongside the talking and the daydreaming. It's funny you ask how it compared, because to me, it all just felt like one big ball of nervous energy. But yes, staying seated? That was torture. Pure torture. I remember in middle school, we had these desks where the chair was attached to the table part, you know the ones? I would rock that whole contraption back and forth, back and forth, until the metal legs would squeak on the linoleum. The teachers hated it. And if I wasn't rocking, I was finding any excuse to get up. I’d drop my pencil on purpose just so I could dive under the desk to get it, or I’d ask to go to the bathroom three times before lunch, or I’d volunteer to take the eraser outside and clap the chalk dust out of it just to get out of that seat. \n\nAnd you know, even now, even when I'm really, really trying my absolute hardest to just sit there and look professional—like in those coaching meetings I mentioned, where I'm trying to look calm and collected—it's like the energy just has to leak out somewhere. If I force my body to stay in the chair, my foot is under the desk, just bouncing away, going a mile a minute. Or I'll have a pen in my hand, and I'll be clicking it. Click, click, click. I don't even realize I'm doing it until the supervisor stops talking and just stares at my hand. It's embarrassing, frankly. I’m a grown man, fifty-seven years old, and I still can't just sit still like a normal person. It’s like, if my mouth isn't moving, my foot has to be, or my fingers have to be messing with something. So, to answer your question, yes, the fidgeting was always a huge part of it, and it still is, even when I'm trying my best to hide it.\n\nDoctor: Looking back at your school years, would you say that this constant motion, the trouble focusing, and the blurting things out were causing real problems for you—like did it affect your grades, your friendships, or get you into trouble with teachers and parents? Or was it more just an annoyance that people commented on?\n\nPatient: Well, looking back at school... I mean, it was definitely more than just a little annoyance, though I wouldn't say I was a juvenile delinquent or anything like that. It was more like... a constant friction, you know? Like I said about the report cards, my grades always took a hit because of the silly mistakes, not because I didn't understand the material. And I spent more than my fair share of time clapping those erasers or sitting in detention because I just couldn't keep my mouth shut. My friends usually thought I was a riot, but the teachers... yeah, I was a headache for them. My poor mother got a lot of phone calls.\n\nBut you know, you get through school, you get out into the real world, and you figure out ways to cope. Or at least, you find jobs where it doesn't matter as much, where you're on your feet and moving. But now? Now it's becoming a very, very real problem. That's the whole reason I finally decided I needed to talk to somebody. Because at this job, sitting at this desk, it's actually starting to show up in my official performance reviews. \n\nAnd I'll tell you, it breaks my heart a little bit, because I am really, really trying. I put in the effort every single day. And the funny thing is, I am great with the customers. I really am! I can talk to anybody, I can calm down a caller who's been on hold for twenty minutes, we end up having a nice chat about their grandkids or the weather in Ohio, and we get along just fine. My customer satisfaction scores are always great. \n\nBut then... there's the administrative side of it. The tickets. We have this whole computerized ticketing system, and my supervisors keep pulling me into those little glass offices and pointing out that I have all these unfinished tickets. I'll take a call, resolve the issue, but then I'll get distracted before I finish typing up the notes, or I'll skip a step in the logging process. Just these careless mistakes, exactly like the ones Mrs. Gable used to yell at me for. But here, it messes up the whole workflow. Because then the next shift comes in, or another department gets the ticket, and they have to stop what they're doing to figure out what I did wrong or fill in the blanks I left. \n\nSo it ends up slowing the entire team down. And it's just so incredibly frustrating to sit in those reviews and hear them tell me I'm holding everyone back, especially when I'm putting in so much effort and doing so well with the actual people on the phone. It's like I can do the hard part—talking to the angry customers—but the supposedly easy part, just filling out the form and finishing the ticket, is what's tripping me up and threatening my job. So, yes, it's causing real problems now. Very real problems.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever been evaluated by a doctor or psychiatrist for ADHD or attention problems, or have you ever been prescribed any medications like stimulants for focus or attention issues?\n\nPatient: No, no, never. Never anything like that. I mean, you have to remember, when I was growing up—going through grade school in the late sixties and the seventies—they didn't really do that sort of thing. If you were bouncing off the walls or talking too much, they didn't send you to a psychiatrist or give you a pill. They just... well, they sent you to the principal's office, or they made you clap the erasers, like I said. Or they just wrote on your report card that you weren't applying yourself. \n\nSo, no, nobody ever sat me down and said, \"Let's test you for an attention problem.\" It just wasn't something people talked about back then, at least not in my neighborhood or my school. And then, as I got older and out into the workforce, I just sort of figured, well, this is just who I am, you know? I'm just a guy who has a little too much nervous energy and gets sidetracked easily. I never really thought of it as a medical thing that you go to a doctor for. \n\nSo I've never been on any medications for it, no stimulants, nothing like that. Honestly, the closest thing I've ever had to medication for focus is just drinking a whole lot of black coffee to try and keep myself on track during the morning shift, but even that doesn't really do the trick when it comes to sitting down and finishing those computer tickets. It's really only been the last few months, with those performance reviews getting worse and my supervisor pulling me into that little glass office more and more, that I finally started thinking, \"Okay, maybe this isn't just a personality quirk. Maybe I actually need to talk to a professional about this.\" \n\nMy wife was the one who finally pushed me to make the appointment, actually. She saw how much it was weighing on me, coming home worried about my job security, and she said, \"You're fifty-seven years old, you've worked hard your whole life, you shouldn't be stressing out this much about a desk job if there's something out there that can help.\" So, I guess to make a very long story short, no, this is the very first time I've ever sat down with a doctor to actually talk about any of this.\n\nDoctor: When you think back to your earliest memories—say, around age five or six, when you first started school—do you remember having this same restless feeling and trouble sitting still, or did it start a bit later?\n\nPatient: Oh, gosh, age five or six? Yeah, absolutely. It definitely didn't just start with this desk job, I can tell you that much for free. Looking back, this whole... whatever you want to call it, this restlessness, it was around long, long before this job change. I mean, it goes all the way back to the very beginning. \n\nI remember being in, what was it, first grade? We had those little wooden chairs, you know the ones, painted in bright primary colors, red and blue and yellow. And I just could not keep all four legs of that little red chair on the floor at the same time. It was like a physical impossibility for me. I was always tipping it back, tapping my toes, looking around at what everyone else was doing. \n\nAnd my mother, bless her heart, she kept all my old report cards in this big cardboard shoebox in the hall closet—she was sentimental like that, kept absolutely everything, every drawing, every spelling test. And if you looked at those grade school report cards, it was the exact same story every single year. The teachers would always write these little notes in the margins. They were very polite, but you could tell they were at their wit's end with me. They would always write that I was a bright boy, that I had a lot of potential, but that I was just so careless. \"Careless\" was the word they loved to use. Bright but careless. \n\nAnd they always, always mentioned the talking. \"Talks out of turn,\" \"Needs to learn to raise his hand,\" \"Distracts his neighbors.\" It was like a broken record. And they were constantly having to remind me to stay with my work. I'd sit down, do the first two addition problems, and then I'd be looking out the window at the custodian mowing the lawn, or I'd be turning around trying to talk to the kid behind me about whatever cartoon was on TV the night before. The teacher would have to walk over, tap my desk, and say my name to get me to look back at the paper. \n\nSo, yeah, it definitely didn't start later in life. It was right there from the very beginning, from the moment they first asked me to sit still and pay attention in a classroom. It's just that back then, it was just seen as boys being boys, or me not applying myself. But that restless, buzzing feeling where you just can't stay on track and keep your mouth shut? Oh, yeah, that's been with me since day one.\n\nDoctor: From the time you finished school and started working as an adult, all the way up until now at fifty-seven, would you say this restlessness and difficulty focusing has been pretty much constant, or were there stretches of time—maybe months or years—where it wasn't as much of a problem for you?\n\nPatient: Well, that's a really good question, because for the longest time, it wasn't really a *problem* problem, if you know what I mean. Like I said, I'd always just been the energetic guy. For decades, I worked jobs where I could move around, or if I was on the phones, I could pace back and forth with a headset, doodle on a notepad, you name it. The restlessness was always there, just sort of humming in the background, but I managed it. I really did. I figured out how to make it work for me.\n\nBut then... gosh, let me think about the exact timeline. It’s been noticeably harder to manage for about, I'd say, a year and ten months now. Yeah, almost exactly a year and ten months. And I know the exact timeframe because that’s when the company decided to do this massive overhaul of our whole department. They brought in all these corporate consultants, and they completely shifted my job into this new—what do they call it—a hybrid chat and CRM role. CRM stands for Customer Relationship Management, in case you aren't familiar with the corporate lingo. It's this incredibly complicated, layered software system. \n\nSo, instead of just talking to a nice lady on the phone and jotting down a quick note like I used to do for years, suddenly I'm supposed to be monitoring two different live chat windows on my left monitor, keeping the main phone line open on my headset, and simultaneously logging every single tiny detail of the interaction into this CRM database on my right monitor. And there are all these drop-down menus, and required fields, and specific tags you have to apply. If you miss one little checkbox, the whole ticket bounces back to you or gets lost in the ether. \n\nAnd that's when the wheels really started to come off the wagon for me. A year and ten months of just... well, drowning in it, to be perfectly honest. Because before that, I could rely on my gift of gab and my people skills to get me through. But this new hybrid setup, it demands a kind of sitting still, staring at a screen, and juggling tiny, boring details that my brain just absolutely rebels against. I try to look at the chat window, and then the phone beeps, and then I forget which drop-down menu I was in, and I end up clicking the wrong thing, and it's just a mess. \n\nSo, yes, the restless feeling and the trouble focusing, that's always been with me, sure. But it wasn't threatening my livelihood until they switched up my job into this hybrid CRM thing a year and ten months ago. It's just a whole different ballgame now, and I just can't seem to get the hang of it no matter how hard I try, which is why those performance reviews have been tanking. It's like they took the one thing I'm bad at and made it my entire job description...\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. I'd sit down, do the first two addition problems, and then I'd be looking out the window at the custodian mowing the lawn, or I'd be turning around trying to talk to the kid behind me about whatever cartoon was on TV the night before. The teacher would have to walk over, tap my desk, and say my name to get me to look back at the paper. \n\nSo, yeah, it definitely didn't start later in life. It was right there from the very beginning, from the moment they first asked me to sit still and pay attention in a classroom. It's just that back then, it was just seen as boys being boys, or me not applying myself. But that restless, buzzing feeling where you just can't stay on track and keep your mouth shut? Oh, yeah, that's been with me since day one.\n\nDoctor: From the time you finished school and started working as an adult, all the way up until now at fifty-seven, would you say this restlessness and difficulty focusing has been pretty much constant, or were there stretches of time—maybe months or years—where it wasn't as much of a problem for you?\n\nPatient: Well, that's a really good question, because for the longest time, it wasn't really a *problem* problem, if you know what I mean. Like I said, I'd always just been the energetic guy. For decades, I worked jobs where I could move around, or if I was on the phones, I could pace back and forth with a headset, doodle on a notepad, you name it. The restlessness was always there, just sort of humming in the background, but I managed it. I really did. I figured out how to make it work for me.\n\nBut then... gosh, let me think about the exact timeline. It’s been noticeably harder to manage for about, I'd say, a year and ten months now. Yeah, almost exactly a year and ten months. And I know the exact timeframe because that’s when the company decided to do this massive overhaul of our whole department. They brought in all these corporate consultants, and they completely shifted my job into this new—what do they call it—a hybrid chat and CRM role. CRM stands for Customer Relationship Management, in case you aren't familiar with the corporate lingo. It's this incredibly complicated, layered software system. \n\nSo, instead of just talking to a nice lady on the phone and jotting down a quick note like I used to do for years, suddenly I'm supposed to be monitoring two different live chat windows on my left monitor, keeping the main phone line open on my headset, and simultaneously logging every single tiny detail of the interaction into this CRM database on my right monitor. And there are all these drop-down menus, and required fields, and specific tags you have to apply. If you miss one little checkbox, the whole ticket bounces back to you or gets lost in the ether. \n\nAnd that's when the wheels really started to come off the wagon for me. A year and ten months of just... well, drowning in it, to be perfectly honest. Because before that, I could rely on my gift of gab and my people skills to get me through. But this new hybrid setup, it demands a kind of sitting still, staring at a screen, and juggling tiny, boring details that my brain just absolutely rebels against. I try to look at the chat window, and then the phone beeps, and then I forget which drop-down menu I was in, and I end up clicking the wrong thing, and it's just a mess. \n\nSo, yes, the restless feeling and the trouble focusing, that's always been with me, sure. But it wasn't threatening my livelihood until they switched up my job into this hybrid CRM thing a year and ten months ago. It's just a whole different ballgame now, and I just can't seem to get the hang of it no matter how hard I try, which is why those performance reviews have been tanking. It's like they took the one thing I'm bad at and made it my entire job description...\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, if I'm being completely honest with you, no, there were never really any stretches where I could just sit down and laser in on something if it didn't naturally grab me. It's always been a struggle, right from the jump, even back in my twenties and thirties. Even when I had those jobs that let me move around and talk to people, if I had to actually sit and read a manual or listen to a long presentation, my brain would just sort of... float away. It's like my attention span has a built-in timer on it, and once it dings, that's it, the oven is off, no matter how hard I try to keep it on.

Take my current job, for instance, since that's what's really bringing this all to a head. They have these coaching meetings now. Oh boy, the coaching meetings. My supervisor—nice guy, really, means well, but he absolutely loves the sound of his own voice—he pulls us into this stuffy little conference room. It’s got these awful fluorescent lights that always have this faint buzzing sound, you know the kind I mean? And he'll put up these endless PowerPoint slides to go over our call metrics or to explain some brand new procedure they're rolling out for the chat system. 

And I really do try to pay attention. I sit there with my yellow legal pad, pen in hand, and I start off fine. I really do. I'm nodding, I'm looking right at him, I'm ready to learn. But after just a few minutes... I don't even know where my mind goes. I'll be reading the new procedure packet they handed out—the ones they print on that cheap paper that always smudges ink on your fingers—and suddenly I realize my eyes kept moving all the way down the page, but I didn't really take in a single word of what was said or what I just read. It's like the words just bounce right off my forehead. I'll get to the bottom of a paragraph and think, "What on earth was that about?" 

And it's the same when someone is talking to me in those longer meetings. I'll be staring right at my supervisor's mouth moving, making eye contact and everything, but the actual information just isn't registering in my brain after the first five minutes. Then I have to scramble to try and figure out what I missed, but by then he's moved on to the next slide, and I'm completely lost. So, to answer your question, no, the focus issue has always been right there alongside the physical restlessness. It just wasn't as glaring of an issue until I got stuck in a job where my whole day is reading procedures and sitting in coaching meetings!

Like, would your attention wander, or did you find it hard to organize your work or remember instructions?\n\nPatient: Well, yes, absolutely. Focusing has always been... well, it's always been a bit of a moving target for me, if I'm being completely honest. Even now, it's not just the sitting still that gets me. Like, they have these coaching meetings at work—these long, drawn-out sessions where the supervisor sits down with you to go over your call metrics, or they hand out these new procedural manuals that are thick as a phone book. The supervisor will be talking, or I'll be reading down the page of this new procedure, and my eyes are still moving across the words, or I'm still nodding at the supervisor, but suddenly I realize I haven't actually taken in a single thing that was just said or read for the last five minutes. My mind has just completely wandered off to what I'm having for lunch, or a noise out in the hallway, or fixing that squeaky hinge on my back door at home. How much of a problem was that compared to the talking and attention issues?\n\nPatient: Oh, the fidgeting. Yes. I mean, it was always there, right alongside the talking and the daydreaming. It's funny you ask how it compared, because to me, it all just felt like one big ball of nervous energy. But yes, staying seated? That was torture. Pure torture. I remember in middle school, we had these desks where the chair was attached to the table part, you know the ones? I would rock that whole contraption back and forth, back and forth, until the metal legs would squeak on the linoleum. The teachers hated it. And if I wasn't rocking, I was finding any excuse to get up. I’d drop my pencil on purpose just so I could dive under the desk to get it, or I’d ask to go to the bathroom three times before lunch, or I’d volunteer to take the eraser outside and clap the chalk dust out of it just to get out of that seat. \n\nAnd you know, even now, even when I'm really, really trying my absolute hardest to just sit there and look professional—like in those coaching meetings I mentioned, where I'm trying to look calm and collected—it's like the energy just has to leak out somewhere. If I force my body to stay in the chair, my foot is under the desk, just bouncing away, going a mile a minute. Or I'll have a pen in my hand, and I'll be clicking it. Click, click, click. I don't even realize I'm doing it until the supervisor stops talking and just stares at my hand. It's embarrassing, frankly. I’m a grown man, fifty-seven years old, and I still can't just sit still like a normal person. It’s like, if my mouth isn't moving, my foot has to be, or my fingers have to be messing with something. Like I said about the report cards, my grades always took a hit because of the silly mistakes, not because I didn't understand the material. And I spent more than my fair share of time clapping those erasers or sitting in detention because I just couldn't keep my mouth shut. So, yes, it's causing real problems now. Very real problems.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever been evaluated by a doctor or psychiatrist for ADHD or attention problems, or have you ever been prescribed any medications like stimulants for focus or attention issues?\n\nPatient: No, no, never. If you were bouncing off the walls or talking too much, they didn't send you to a psychiatrist or give you a pill. They just... well, they sent you to the principal's office, or they made you clap the erasers, like I said. Yeah, absolutely. It definitely didn't just start with this desk job, I can tell you that much for free. I mean, it goes all the way back to the very beginning. \n\nI remember being in, what was it, first grade? We had those little wooden chairs, you know the ones, painted in bright primary colors, red and blue and yellow. And I just could not keep all four legs of that little red chair on the floor at the same time. It was like a physical impossibility for me. I was always tipping it back, tapping my toes, looking around at what everyone else was doing. \n\nAnd my mother, bless her heart, she kept all my old report cards in this big cardboard shoebox in the hall closet—she was sentimental like that, kept absolutely everything, every drawing, every spelling test. And if you looked at those grade school report cards, it was the exact same story every single year. The teachers would always write these little notes in the margins. They were very polite, but you could tell they were at their wit's end with me. And they were constantly having to remind me to stay with my work. I'd sit down, do the first two addition problems, and then I'd be looking out the window at the custodian mowing the lawn, or I'd be turning around trying to talk to the kid behind me about whatever cartoon was on TV the night before. The teacher would have to walk over, tap my desk, and say my name to get me to look back at the paper. \n\nSo, yeah, it definitely didn't start later in life. It was right there from the very beginning, from the moment they first asked me to sit still and pay attention in a classroom. It's just that back then, it was just seen as boys being boys, or me not applying myself. But that restless, buzzing feeling where you just can't stay on track and keep your mouth shut? Oh, yeah, that's been with me since day one.\n\nDoctor: From the time you finished school and started working as an adult, all the way up until now at fifty-seven, would you say this restlessness and difficulty focusing has been pretty much constant, or were there stretches of time—maybe months or years—where it wasn't as much of a problem for you?\n\nPatient: Well, that's a really good question, because for the longest time, it wasn't really a *problem* problem, if you know what I mean. Like I said, I'd always just been the energetic guy. For decades, I worked jobs where I could move around, or if I was on the phones, I could pace back and forth with a headset, doodle on a notepad, you name it. The restlessness was always there, just sort of humming in the background, but I managed it. I really did. I figured out how to make it work for me.\n\nBut then... gosh, let me think about the exact timeline. It’s been noticeably harder to manage for about, I'd say, a year and ten months now. Yeah, almost exactly a year and ten months. And I know the exact timeframe because that’s when the company decided to do this massive overhaul of our whole department. They brought in all these corporate consultants, and they completely shifted my job into this new—what do they call it—a hybrid chat and CRM role. CRM stands for Customer Relationship Management, in case you aren't familiar with the corporate lingo. It's this incredibly complicated, layered software system. \n\nSo, instead of just talking to a nice lady on the phone and jotting down a quick note like I used to do for years, suddenly I'm supposed to be monitoring two different live chat windows on my left monitor, keeping the main phone line open on my headset, and simultaneously logging every single tiny detail of the interaction into this CRM database on my right monitor. And there are all these drop-down menus, and required fields, and specific tags you have to apply. If you miss one little checkbox, the whole ticket bounces back to you or gets lost in the ether. \n\nAnd that's when the wheels really started to come off the wagon for me. A year and ten months of just... well, drowning in it, to be perfectly honest. Because before that, I could rely on my gift of gab and my people skills to get me through. But this new hybrid setup, it demands a kind of sitting still, staring at a screen, and juggling tiny, boring details that my brain just absolutely rebels against. I try to look at the chat window, and then the phone beeps, and then I forget which drop-down menu I was in, and I end up clicking the wrong thing, and it's just a mess. \n\nSo, yes, the restless feeling and the trouble focusing, that's always been with me, sure. It's just a whole different ballgame now, and I just can't seem to get the hang of it no matter how hard I try, which is why those performance reviews have been tanking. It's like they took the one thing I'm bad at and made it my entire job description...\n\nDoctor: You mentioned that before this job change, the restlessness was manageable and you found jobs that worked with your style. I don't even realize I'm doing it until the supervisor stops talking and just stares at my hand. It's embarrassing, frankly. I’m a grown man, fifty-seven years old, and I still can't just sit still like a normal person. It’s like, if my mouth isn't moving, my foot has to be, or my fingers have to be messing with something. So, to answer your question, yes, the fidgeting was always a huge part of it, and it still is, even when I'm trying my best to hide it.\n\nDoctor: Looking back at your school years, would you say that this constant motion, the trouble focusing, and the blurting things out were causing real problems for you—like did it affect your grades, your friendships, or get you into trouble with teachers and parents? Or was it more just an annoyance that people commented on?\n\nPatient: Well, looking back at school... I mean, it was definitely more than just a little annoyance, though I wouldn't say I was a juvenile delinquent or anything like that. It was more like... a constant friction, you know? Like I said about the report cards, my grades always took a hit because of the silly mistakes, not because I didn't understand the material. And I spent more than my fair share of time clapping those erasers or sitting in detention because I just couldn't keep my mouth shut. My friends usually thought I was a riot, but the teachers... yeah, I was a headache for them. My poor mother got a lot of phone calls.\n\nBut you know, you get through school, you get out into the real world, and you figure out ways to cope. Or at least, you find jobs where it doesn't matter as much, where you're on your feet and moving. But now? Now it's becoming a very, very real problem. That's the whole reason I finally decided I needed to talk to somebody. Because at this job, sitting at this desk, it's actually starting to show up in my official performance reviews. \n\nAnd I'll tell you, it breaks my heart a little bit, because I am really, really trying. I put in the effort every single day. And the funny thing is, I am great with the customers. I really am! I can talk to anybody, I can calm down a caller who's been on hold for twenty minutes, we end up having a nice chat about their grandkids or the weather in Ohio, and we get along just fine. My customer satisfaction scores are always great. \n\nBut then... there's the administrative side of it. The tickets. We have this whole computerized ticketing system, and my supervisors keep pulling me into those little glass offices and pointing out that I have all these unfinished tickets. I'll take a call, resolve the issue, but then I'll get distracted before I finish typing up the notes, or I'll skip a step in the logging process. Just these careless mistakes, exactly like the ones Mrs. Gable used to yell at me for. But here, it messes up the whole workflow. Because then the next shift comes in, or another department gets the ticket, and they have to stop what they're doing to figure out what I did wrong or fill in the blanks I left. \n\nSo it ends up slowing the entire team down. And it's just so incredibly frustrating to sit in those reviews and hear them tell me I'm holding everyone back, especially when I'm putting in so much effort and doing so well with the actual people on the phone. It's like I can do the hard part—talking to the angry customers—but the supposedly easy part, just filling out the form and finishing the ticket, is what's tripping me up and threatening my job. So, yes, it's causing real problems now. Very real problems.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever been evaluated by a doctor or psychiatrist for ADHD or attention problems, or have you ever been prescribed any medications like stimulants for focus or attention issues?\n\nPatient: No, no, never. Never anything like that. I mean, you have to remember, when I was growing up—going through grade school in the late sixties and the seventies—they didn't really do that sort of thing. If you were bouncing off the walls or talking too much, they didn't send you to a psychiatrist or give you a pill. They just... well, they sent you to the principal's office, or they made you clap the erasers, like I said. Or they just wrote on your report card that you weren't applying yourself. \n\nSo, no, nobody ever sat me down and said, \"Let's test you for an attention problem.\" It just wasn't something people talked about back then, at least not in my neighborhood or my school. And then, as I got older and out into the workforce, I just sort of figured, well, this is just who I am, you know? I'm just a guy who has a little too much nervous energy and gets sidetracked easily. I never really thought of it as a medical thing that you go to a doctor for. \n\nSo I've never been on any medications for it, no stimulants, nothing like that. Honestly, the closest thing I've ever had to medication for focus is just drinking a whole lot of black coffee to try and keep myself on track during the morning shift, but even that doesn't really do the trick when it comes to sitting down and finishing those computer tickets. It's really only been the last few months, with those performance reviews getting worse and my supervisor pulling me into that little glass office more and more, that I finally started thinking, \"Okay, maybe this isn't just a personality quirk. Maybe I actually need to talk to a professional about this.\" \n\nMy wife was the one who finally pushed me to make the appointment, actually. She saw how much it was weighing on me, coming home worried about my job security, and she said, \"You're fifty-seven years old, you've worked hard your whole life, you shouldn't be stressing out this much about a desk job if there's something out there that can help.\" So, I guess to make a very long story short, no, this is the very first time I've ever sat down with a doctor to actually talk about any of this.\n\nDoctor: When you think back to your earliest memories—say, around age five or six, when you first started school—do you remember having this same restless feeling and trouble sitting still, or did it start a bit later?\n\nPatient: Oh, gosh, age five or six? Yeah, absolutely. It definitely didn't just start with this desk job, I can tell you that much for free. Looking back, this whole... whatever you want to call it, this restlessness, it was around long, long before this job change. I mean, it goes all the way back to the very beginning. \n\nI remember being in, what was it, first grade? We had those little wooden chairs, you know the ones, painted in bright primary colors, red and blue and yellow. And I just could not keep all four legs of that little red chair on the floor at the same time. It was like a physical impossibility for me. I was always tipping it back, tapping my toes, looking around at what everyone else was doing. \n\nAnd my mother, bless her heart, she kept all my old report cards in this big cardboard shoebox in the hall closet—she was sentimental like that, kept absolutely everything, every drawing, every spelling test. And if you looked at those grade school report cards, it was the exact same story every single year. The teachers would always write these little notes in the margins. They were very polite, but you could tell they were at their wit's end with me. They would always write that I was a bright boy, that I had a lot of potential, but that I was just so careless. \"Careless\" was the word they loved to use. Bright but careless. \n\nAnd they always, always mentioned the talking. \"Talks out of turn,\" \"Needs to learn to raise his hand,\" \"Distracts his neighbors.\" It was like a broken record. And they were constantly having to remind me to stay with my work. I'd sit down, do the first two addition problems, and then I'd be looking out the window at the custodian mowing the lawn, or I'd be turning around trying to talk to the kid behind me about whatever cartoon was on TV the night before. The teacher would have to walk over, tap my desk, and say my name to get me to look back at the paper. \n\nSo, yeah, it definitely didn't start later in life. It was right there from the very beginning, from the moment they first asked me to sit still and pay attention in a classroom. It's just that back then, it was just seen as boys being boys, or me not applying myself. But that restless, buzzing feeling where you just can't stay on track and keep your mouth shut? Oh, yeah, that's been with me since day one.\n\nDoctor: From the time you finished school and started working as an adult, all the way up until now at fifty-seven, would you say this restlessness and difficulty focusing has been pretty much constant, or were there stretches of time—maybe months or years—where it wasn't as much of a problem for you?\n\nPatient: Well, that's a really good question, because for the longest time, it wasn't really a *problem* problem, if you know what I mean. Like I said, I'd always just been the energetic guy. For decades, I worked jobs where I could move around, or if I was on the phones, I could pace back and forth with a headset, doodle on a notepad, you name it. The restlessness was always there, just sort of humming in the background, but I managed it. I really did. I figured out how to make it work for me.\n\nBut then... gosh, let me think about the exact timeline. It’s been noticeably harder to manage for about, I'd say, a year and ten months now. Yeah, almost exactly a year and ten months. And I know the exact timeframe because that’s when the company decided to do this massive overhaul of our whole department. They brought in all these corporate consultants, and they completely shifted my job into this new—what do they call it—a hybrid chat and CRM role. CRM stands for Customer Relationship Management, in case you aren't familiar with the corporate lingo. It's this incredibly complicated, layered software system. \n\nSo, instead of just talking to a nice lady on the phone and jotting down a quick note like I used to do for years, suddenly I'm supposed to be monitoring two different live chat windows on my left monitor, keeping the main phone line open on my headset, and simultaneously logging every single tiny detail of the interaction into this CRM database on my right monitor. And there are all these drop-down menus, and required fields, and specific tags you have to apply. If you miss one little checkbox, the whole ticket bounces back to you or gets lost in the ether. \n\nAnd that's when the wheels really started to come off the wagon for me. A year and ten months of just... well, drowning in it, to be perfectly honest. Because before that, I could rely on my gift of gab and my people skills to get me through. But this new hybrid setup, it demands a kind of sitting still, staring at a screen, and juggling tiny, boring details that my brain just absolutely rebels against. I try to look at the chat window, and then the phone beeps, and then I forget which drop-down menu I was in, and I end up clicking the wrong thing, and it's just a mess. \n\nSo, yes, the restless feeling and the trouble focusing, that's always been with me, sure. But it wasn't threatening my livelihood until they switched up my job into this hybrid CRM thing a year and ten months ago. It's just a whole different ballgame now, and I just can't seem to get the hang of it no matter how hard I try, which is why those performance reviews have been tanking. It's like they took the one thing I'm bad at and made it my entire job description...\n\nDoctor: You mentioned that before this job change, the restlessness was manageable and you found jobs that worked with your style. But I want to ask specifically about your ability to focus and pay attention—were there ever stretches of time, even a few months or a year or two, when you felt like you could sit down and concentrate on something without your mind wandering, or has the difficulty focusing on tasks always been there since childhood, just like the restlessness?\n\nPatient: Well, if I'm being completely honest with you, no, there were never really any stretches where I could just sit down and laser in on something if it didn't naturally grab me. It's always been a struggle, right from the jump, even back in my twenties and thirties. Even when I had those jobs that let me move around and talk to people, if I had to actually sit and read a manual or listen to a long presentation, my brain would just sort of... float away. It's like my attention span has a built-in timer on it, and once it dings, that's it, the oven is off, no matter how hard I try to keep it on.\n\nTake my current job, for instance, since that's what's really bringing this all to a head. They have these coaching meetings now. Oh boy, the coaching meetings. My supervisor—nice guy, really, means well, but he absolutely loves the sound of his own voice—he pulls us into this stuffy little conference room. It’s got these awful fluorescent lights that always have this faint buzzing sound, you know the kind I mean? And he'll put up these endless PowerPoint slides to go over our call metrics or to explain some brand new procedure they're rolling out for the chat system. \n\nAnd I really do try to pay attention. I sit there with my yellow legal pad, pen in hand, and I start off fine. I really do. I'm nodding, I'm looking right at him, I'm ready to learn. But after just a few minutes... I don't even know where my mind goes. I'll be reading the new procedure packet they handed out—the ones they print on that cheap paper that always smudges ink on your fingers—and suddenly I realize my eyes kept moving all the way down the page, but I didn't really take in a single word of what was said or what I just read. It's like the words just bounce right off my forehead. I'll get to the bottom of a paragraph and think, \"What on earth was that about?\" \n\nAnd it's the same when someone is talking to me in those longer meetings. I'll be staring right at my supervisor's mouth moving, making eye contact and everything, but the actual information just isn't registering in my brain after the first five minutes. Then I have to scramble to try and figure out what I missed, but by then he's moved on to the next slide, and I'm completely lost. So, to answer your question, no, the focus issue has always been right there alongside the physical restlessness. It just wasn't as glaring of an issue until I got stuck in a job where my whole day is reading procedures and sitting in coaching meetings!\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"

I was always tipping it back, tapping my toes, looking around at what everyone else was doing. \n\nAnd my mother, bless her heart, she kept all my old report cards in this big cardboard shoebox in the hall closet—she was sentimental like that, kept absolutely everything, every drawing, every spelling test. And if you looked at those grade school report cards, it was the exact same story every single year. The teachers would always write these little notes in the margins. They were very polite, but you could tell they were at their wit's end with me. They would always write that I was a bright boy, that I had a lot of potential, but that I was just so careless. \"Careless\" was the word they loved to use. Bright but careless. \n\nAnd they always, always mentioned the talking. \"Talks out of turn,\" \"Needs to learn to raise his hand,\" \"Distracts his neighbors.\" It was like a broken record. And they were constantly having to remind me to stay with my work. I'd sit down, do the first two addition problems, and then I'd be looking out the window at the custodian mowing the lawn, or I'd be turning around trying to talk to the kid behind me about whatever cartoon was on TV the night before. The teacher would have to walk over, tap my desk, and say my name to get me to look back at the paper. \n\nSo, yeah, it definitely didn't start later in life. It was right there from the very beginning, from the moment they first asked me to sit still and pay attention in a classroom. It's just that back then, it was just seen as boys being boys, or me not applying myself. But that restless, buzzing feeling where you just can't stay on track and keep your mouth shut? Oh, yeah, that's been with me since day one.\n\nDoctor: From the time you finished school and started working as an adult, all the way up until now at fifty-seven, would you say this restlessness and difficulty focusing has been pretty much constant, or were there stretches of time—maybe months or years—where it wasn't as much of a problem for you?\n\nPatient: Well, that's a really good question, because for the longest time, it wasn't really a *problem* problem, if you know what I mean. Like I said, I'd always just been the energetic guy. For decades, I worked jobs where I could move around, or if I was on the phones, I could pace back and forth with a headset, doodle on a notepad, you name it. The restlessness was always there, just sort of humming in the background, but I managed it. I really did. I figured out how to make it work for me.\n\nBut then... gosh, let me think about the exact timeline. It’s been noticeably harder to manage for about, I'd say, a year and ten months now. Yeah, almost exactly a year and ten months. And I know the exact timeframe because that’s when the company decided to do this massive overhaul of our whole department. They brought in all these corporate consultants, and they completely shifted my job into this new—what do they call it—a hybrid chat and CRM role. CRM stands for Customer Relationship Management, in case you aren't familiar with the corporate lingo. It's this incredibly complicated, layered software system. \n\nSo, instead of just talking to a nice lady on the phone and jotting down a quick note like I used to do for years, suddenly I'm supposed to be monitoring two different live chat windows on my left monitor, keeping the main phone line open on my headset, and simultaneously logging every single tiny detail of the interaction into this CRM database on my right monitor. And there are all these drop-down menus, and required fields, and specific tags you have to apply. If you miss one little checkbox, the whole ticket bounces back to you or gets lost in the ether. \n\nAnd that's when the wheels really started to come off the wagon for me. A year and ten months of just... well, drowning in it, to be perfectly honest. Because before that, I could rely on my gift of gab and my people skills to get me through. But this new hybrid setup, it demands a kind of sitting still, staring at a screen, and juggling tiny, boring details that my brain just absolutely rebels against. I try to look at the chat window, and then the phone beeps, and then I forget which drop-down menu I was in, and I end up clicking the wrong thing, and it's just a mess. \n\nSo, yes, the restless feeling and the trouble focusing, that's always been with me, sure. But it wasn't threatening my livelihood until they switched up my job into this hybrid CRM thing a year and ten months ago. It's just a whole different ballgame now, and I just can't seem to get the hang of it no matter how hard I try, which is why those performance reviews have been tanking. It's like they took the one thing I'm bad at and made it my entire job description...\n\nDoctor: You mentioned that before this job change, the restlessness was manageable and you found jobs that worked with your style. But I want to ask specifically about your ability to focus and pay attention—were there ever stretches of time, even a few months or a year or two, when you felt like you could sit down and concentrate on something without your mind wandering, or has the difficulty focusing on tasks always been there since childhood, just like the restlessness?\n\nPatient: Well, if I'm being completely honest with you, no, there were never really any stretches where I could just sit down and laser in on something if it didn't naturally grab me. It's always been a struggle, right from the jump, even back in my twenties and thirties. Even when I had those jobs that let me move around and talk to people, if I had to actually sit and read a manual or listen to a long presentation, my brain would just sort of... float away. It's like my attention span has a built-in timer on it, and once it dings, that's it, the oven is off, no matter how hard I try to keep it on.\n\nTake my current job, for instance, since that's what's really bringing this all to a head. They have these coaching meetings now. Oh boy, the coaching meetings. My supervisor—nice guy, really, means well, but he absolutely loves the sound of his own voice—he pulls us into this stuffy little conference room. It’s got these awful fluorescent lights that always have this faint buzzing sound, you know the kind I mean? And he'll put up these endless PowerPoint slides to go over our call metrics or to explain some brand new procedure they're rolling out for the chat system. \n\nAnd I really do try to pay attention. I sit there with my yellow legal pad, pen in hand, and I start off fine. I really do. I'm nodding, I'm looking right at him, I'm ready to learn. But after just a few minutes... I don't even know where my mind goes. I'll be reading the new procedure packet they handed out—the ones they print on that cheap paper that always smudges ink on your fingers—and suddenly I realize my eyes kept moving all the way down the page, but I didn't really take in a single word of what was said or what I just read. It's like the words just bounce right off my forehead. I'll get to the bottom of a paragraph and think, \"What on earth was that about?\" \n\nAnd it's the same when someone is talking to me in those longer meetings. I'll be staring right at my supervisor's mouth moving, making eye contact and everything, but the actual information just isn't registering in my brain after the first five minutes. Then I have to scramble to try and figure out what I missed, but by then he's moved on to the next slide, and I'm completely lost. So, to answer your question, no, the focus issue has always been right there alongside the physical restlessness. It just wasn't as glaring of an issue until I got stuck in a job where my whole day is reading procedures and sitting in coaching meetings!\n\n[Last Question]\nHave there ever been times in your life—maybe lasting a few days or a week or two—when you felt unusually energetic or in a really great mood, like you needed less sleep than normal but still felt great, or like your thoughts were racing faster than usual? Or times when you felt like you could do anything and took on a bunch of projects all at once?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 57 / customer_service_representative / high_school\n- severity: Mild\n\n[Targeted Info]\nNone\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Verbose patient.\nA verbose patient may\n1) provide excessively detailed responses when describing their symptoms and experiences, even when the details are directly relevant,\n2) elaborate extensively on the background, events, thoughts, feelings, and personal experiences surrounding their symptoms, and\n3) demonstrate difficulty allowing the doctor to guide the interview or move on to another clinical topic.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Oh, I see what you're asking. Like, do I suddenly turn into Superman for a week and try to conquer the world on two hours of sleep? No, no, gosh no. Nothing like that. 

It's actually funny you mention the sleep thing, because my wife—she’s a terribly light sleeper, has to have the room pitch black, she's got this little white noise machine that sounds like a babbling brook, the whole nine yards—she always says that once my head hits the pillow, I am out like a light. I absolutely need my sleep. Always have. If I don't get my solid seven or eight hours, I am just a grumpy old bear the next day, not bouncing off the walls with energy. I'll be dragging my feet, drinking three cups of black coffee just to get through the morning shift. So, no, I've never had these phases where I just stop sleeping and feel perfectly fine. 

And as for the mood or taking on a bunch of projects... well, like I said, my mind is always a bit busy, always kind of humming along, but it's a steady hum. Like an old refrigerator. It's not like a sudden explosion where I wake up on a Tuesday and decide I'm going to remodel the entire kitchen, write a novel, and learn how to speak French all at the same time. I mean, sure, I might get a wild hair and decide to finally organize the workbench in the garage on a Saturday, but that's just normal weekend chore stuff, you know? I might get distracted halfway through and start messing with the lawnmower spark plugs instead, but I'm not taking on the world. 

My energy level, this fidgety, restless feeling I've been telling you about, it's just my baseline. It’s everyday life for me, 365 days a year. It doesn’t come in these big, dramatic waves where I feel invincible or where my thoughts are racing a mile a minute in a way that feels totally out of the ordinary. I'm just a regular guy who can't sit still in a stuffy coaching meeting, not someone going on these extreme, weeks-long joyrides in my own head. So, yeah, I really haven't experienced anything like what you're describing there. Just the same old constant fidgeting I've had since I was sitting in those little wooden chairs in first grade.

It’s got these awful fluorescent lights that always have this faint buzzing sound, you know the kind I mean? And he'll put up these endless PowerPoint slides to go over our call metrics or to explain some brand new procedure they're rolling out for the chat system. \n\nAnd I really do try to pay attention. I sit there with my yellow legal pad, pen in hand, and I start off fine. I really do. I'm nodding, I'm looking right at him, I'm ready to learn. But after just a few minutes... I don't even know where my mind goes. I'll be reading the new procedure packet they handed out—the ones they print on that cheap paper that always smudges ink on your fingers—and suddenly I realize my eyes kept moving all the way down the page, but I didn't really take in a single word of what was said or what I just read. It's like the words just bounce right off my forehead. I'll get to the bottom of a paragraph and think, \"What on earth was that about?\" \n\nAnd it's the same when someone is talking to me in those longer meetings. I'll be staring right at my supervisor's mouth moving, making eye contact and everything, but the actual information just isn't registering in my brain after the first five minutes. Then I have to scramble to try and figure out what I missed, but by then he's moved on to the next slide, and I'm completely lost. So, to answer your question, no, the focus issue has always been right there alongside the physical restlessness. It just wasn't as glaring of an issue until I got stuck in a job where my whole day is reading procedures and sitting in coaching meetings!\n\nDoctor: Have there ever been times in your life—maybe lasting a few days or a week or two—when you felt unusually energetic or in a really great mood, like you needed less sleep than normal but still felt great, or like your thoughts were racing faster than usual? Nothing like that. \n\nIt's actually funny you mention the sleep thing, because my wife—she’s a terribly light sleeper, has to have the room pitch black, she's got this little white noise machine that sounds like a babbling brook, the whole nine yards—she always says that once my head hits the pillow, I am out like a light. I absolutely need my sleep. Always have. If I don't get my solid seven or eight hours, I am just a grumpy old bear the next day, not bouncing off the walls with energy. I'll be dragging my feet, drinking three cups of black coffee just to get through the morning shift. So, no, I've never had these phases where I just stop sleeping and feel perfectly fine. \n\nAnd as for the mood or taking on a bunch of projects... well, like I said, my mind is always a bit busy, always kind of humming along, but it's a steady hum. Like an old refrigerator. It's not like a sudden explosion where I wake up on a Tuesday and decide I'm going to remodel the entire kitchen, write a novel, and learn how to speak French all at the same time. I mean, sure, I might get a wild hair and decide to finally organize the workbench in the garage on a Saturday, but that's just normal weekend chore stuff, you know? I might get distracted halfway through and start messing with the lawnmower spark plugs instead, but I'm not taking on the world. \n\nMy energy level, this fidgety, restless feeling I've been telling you about, it's just my baseline. It’s everyday life for me, 365 days a year. It doesn’t come in these big, dramatic waves where I feel invincible or where my thoughts are racing a mile a minute in a way that feels totally out of the ordinary. I'm just a regular guy who can't sit still in a stuffy coaching meeting, not someone going on these extreme, weeks-long joyrides in my own head. Just the same old constant fidgeting I've had since I was sitting in those little wooden chairs in first grade.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. Like I said, I'd always just been the energetic guy. For decades, I worked jobs where I could move around, or if I was on the phones, I could pace back and forth with a headset, doodle on a notepad, you name it. The restlessness was always there, just sort of humming in the background, but I managed it. I really did. I figured out how to make it work for me.\n\nBut then... gosh, let me think about the exact timeline. It’s been noticeably harder to manage for about, I'd say, a year and ten months now. Yeah, almost exactly a year and ten months. And I know the exact timeframe because that’s when the company decided to do this massive overhaul of our whole department. They brought in all these corporate consultants, and they completely shifted my job into this new—what do they call it—a hybrid chat and CRM role. CRM stands for Customer Relationship Management, in case you aren't familiar with the corporate lingo. It's this incredibly complicated, layered software system. \n\nSo, instead of just talking to a nice lady on the phone and jotting down a quick note like I used to do for years, suddenly I'm supposed to be monitoring two different live chat windows on my left monitor, keeping the main phone line open on my headset, and simultaneously logging every single tiny detail of the interaction into this CRM database on my right monitor. And there are all these drop-down menus, and required fields, and specific tags you have to apply. If you miss one little checkbox, the whole ticket bounces back to you or gets lost in the ether. \n\nAnd that's when the wheels really started to come off the wagon for me. A year and ten months of just... well, drowning in it, to be perfectly honest. Because before that, I could rely on my gift of gab and my people skills to get me through. But this new hybrid setup, it demands a kind of sitting still, staring at a screen, and juggling tiny, boring details that my brain just absolutely rebels against. I try to look at the chat window, and then the phone beeps, and then I forget which drop-down menu I was in, and I end up clicking the wrong thing, and it's just a mess. \n\nSo, yes, the restless feeling and the trouble focusing, that's always been with me, sure. But it wasn't threatening my livelihood until they switched up my job into this hybrid CRM thing a year and ten months ago. It's just a whole different ballgame now, and I just can't seem to get the hang of it no matter how hard I try, which is why those performance reviews have been tanking. It's like they took the one thing I'm bad at and made it my entire job description...\n\nDoctor: You mentioned that before this job change, the restlessness was manageable and you found jobs that worked with your style. But I want to ask specifically about your ability to focus and pay attention—were there ever stretches of time, even a few months or a year or two, when you felt like you could sit down and concentrate on something without your mind wandering, or has the difficulty focusing on tasks always been there since childhood, just like the restlessness?\n\nPatient: Well, if I'm being completely honest with you, no, there were never really any stretches where I could just sit down and laser in on something if it didn't naturally grab me. It's always been a struggle, right from the jump, even back in my twenties and thirties. Even when I had those jobs that let me move around and talk to people, if I had to actually sit and read a manual or listen to a long presentation, my brain would just sort of... float away. It's like my attention span has a built-in timer on it, and once it dings, that's it, the oven is off, no matter how hard I try to keep it on.\n\nTake my current job, for instance, since that's what's really bringing this all to a head. They have these coaching meetings now. Oh boy, the coaching meetings. My supervisor—nice guy, really, means well, but he absolutely loves the sound of his own voice—he pulls us into this stuffy little conference room. It’s got these awful fluorescent lights that always have this faint buzzing sound, you know the kind I mean? And he'll put up these endless PowerPoint slides to go over our call metrics or to explain some brand new procedure they're rolling out for the chat system. \n\nAnd I really do try to pay attention. I sit there with my yellow legal pad, pen in hand, and I start off fine. I really do. I'm nodding, I'm looking right at him, I'm ready to learn. But after just a few minutes... I don't even know where my mind goes. I'll be reading the new procedure packet they handed out—the ones they print on that cheap paper that always smudges ink on your fingers—and suddenly I realize my eyes kept moving all the way down the page, but I didn't really take in a single word of what was said or what I just read. It's like the words just bounce right off my forehead. I'll get to the bottom of a paragraph and think, \"What on earth was that about?\" \n\nAnd it's the same when someone is talking to me in those longer meetings. I'll be staring right at my supervisor's mouth moving, making eye contact and everything, but the actual information just isn't registering in my brain after the first five minutes. Then I have to scramble to try and figure out what I missed, but by then he's moved on to the next slide, and I'm completely lost. So, to answer your question, no, the focus issue has always been right there alongside the physical restlessness. It just wasn't as glaring of an issue until I got stuck in a job where my whole day is reading procedures and sitting in coaching meetings!\n\nDoctor: Have there ever been times in your life—maybe lasting a few days or a week or two—when you felt unusually energetic or in a really great mood, like you needed less sleep than normal but still felt great, or like your thoughts were racing faster than usual? Or times when you felt like you could do anything and took on a bunch of projects all at once?\n\nPatient: Oh, I see what you're asking. Like, do I suddenly turn into Superman for a week and try to conquer the world on two hours of sleep? No, no, gosh no. Nothing like that. \n\nIt's actually funny you mention the sleep thing, because my wife—she’s a terribly light sleeper, has to have the room pitch black, she's got this little white noise machine that sounds like a babbling brook, the whole nine yards—she always says that once my head hits the pillow, I am out like a light. I absolutely need my sleep. Always have. If I don't get my solid seven or eight hours, I am just a grumpy old bear the next day, not bouncing off the walls with energy. I'll be dragging my feet, drinking three cups of black coffee just to get through the morning shift. So, no, I've never had these phases where I just stop sleeping and feel perfectly fine. \n\nAnd as for the mood or taking on a bunch of projects... well, like I said, my mind is always a bit busy, always kind of humming along, but it's a steady hum. Like an old refrigerator. It's not like a sudden explosion where I wake up on a Tuesday and decide I'm going to remodel the entire kitchen, write a novel, and learn how to speak French all at the same time. I mean, sure, I might get a wild hair and decide to finally organize the workbench in the garage on a Saturday, but that's just normal weekend chore stuff, you know? I might get distracted halfway through and start messing with the lawnmower spark plugs instead, but I'm not taking on the world. \n\nMy energy level, this fidgety, restless feeling I've been telling you about, it's just my baseline. It’s everyday life for me, 365 days a year. It doesn’t come in these big, dramatic waves where I feel invincible or where my thoughts are racing a mile a minute in a way that feels totally out of the ordinary. I'm just a regular guy who can't sit still in a stuffy coaching meeting, not someone going on these extreme, weeks-long joyrides in my own head. So, yeah, I really haven't experienced anything like what you're describing there. Just the same old constant fidgeting I've had since I was sitting in those little wooden chairs in first grade.\n\n[Last Question]\nBeyond the problems at work and in school that you've mentioned, have these attention and restlessness issues ever caused you real difficulties in your personal relationships—with your wife, family, or friends—or in taking care of things at home?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, you know, it’s funny you ask about my wife, because... well, bless her heart, we’ve been married for thirty-one years next May, and she is a saint, she really is. But this whole thing definitely causes some friction at home. Not, you know, screaming matches or anything like that, but... well, it’s the quiet time. That's the real sticking point for us. She’s the kind of person who unwinds by just being still. But for me, quiet stuff that’s supposed to relax me—like sitting down to read a book in the living room or trying to get through a movie with her on a Friday night—it just doesn’t work that way. It really doesn't last long at all.

We’ll sit down on the couch, right? She’s got her cup of chamomile tea, she puts on one of those long, slow historical dramas she loves, and I really do try to just relax with her. I want to be a good husband. But give it maybe fifteen, twenty minutes tops, and I’m already shifting around, adjusting the throw pillows, crossing and uncrossing my legs. Then I start commenting on the movie, you know, pointing out that the cars in the background don't match the time period, or asking why the main character didn't just walk out the back door, and she’ll just give me this heavy sigh and pause the TV. 

Or if we’re just supposed to be reading—she loves those cozy mystery novels, the ones with the little cats on the cover—I'll be sitting there in the quiet, and the silence is just... it's loud, if that makes any sense. I just can't take it. I end up getting up for something almost immediately. I'll say I'm just going to get a glass of ice water, but then I'm in the kitchen, and I notice the dog's bowl needs washing, and then I decide to wipe down the counters, and maybe I'll check to see if the porch light bulb needs replacing, and suddenly I've been up for half an hour making a racket while she's trying to enjoy her book. 

So, yeah, it definitely gets on her nerves. She always says, "Can't you just sit and be still with me for one single evening?" And I want to, I really do, but that quiet, relaxing stuff just makes me feel like I'm wearing a sweater that's too tight. I just have to be doing something or moving or talking. So it does put a bit of a strain on those quiet evenings at home, for sure.

    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient.
